(In the matter of the dispute for adjudication under 
section 10(lXd) of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947 between the workmen and the management 
of Indian Overseas Bank, Madras). 

BETWEEN 

Th# General Secretary, All India Overseas Bank Em- 
ployees Union, 151, Mount Road, Madras-2. 

AND 

The General Manager, Indian Overseas Bank, No. 151, 
Mount Road, Madra»-2. 

Rdsracs: 


Order No. L. 120 12/66 /72-LRJII dated 26-8-1972 of 
the Ministry of Labour and Rehabilitation, Depart- 
ment of Labour and Employment, Government of 
India, New Delhi. 

This dispute coming on for final hearing on Wednesday 
the 17th day of January, 1973, upon perusing the reference, 
claim and counter statements and all other material papers 
on record and upon hearing the arguments of Thiruvalargal 
A. L. Somayaji and R. Jamal Mazeem for Thiru B. R. 
Dolia, advocate for the union and of Thiruvalargal 
C. Ramakrishna and N. G, R. Prasad, advocates for the 
management and having stood over till this day for consi- 
deration, this Tribunal made the following 


AWARD 

The issue involved in the industrial dispute between the 
management of the Indian Overseas Bank and their work- 
men referred for adjudication, is as follows:— 

“Whether the demands of he All India Overseas Bank 
Employees Union, Madras for payment of Travel- 
ling and Daily Allowances for three days from 
19th October to 21st October, 1971 to Shri 
Sankaralingam for giving evidence in domestic In- 
quiry. and for treatment of the above period of 
absence as duty are justified? If not, to what re- 
liefs are the workman entitled?*' 

(2) One Sri S. Venugopal employed m the bank at Madras 
was charge-sheeted by the Management for having man- 
handled a co-employee In the bank’s premises. The employee 
was informed by the Enquiry Officer Sri D. I. Ambrose, 
52 G of 1/72 — 15 


by his letter dated 10th December, 1970 (Ex. M-J) that the 
enquiry into the charge was to be held at the Central Office 
at Madras on 17-12-1970 and that he should appear for the 
enquiry and let in evidence, if he had any in nis defence. 
The President of the All India Overseas Bank Employees’ 
Union who was defending the employee wrote a letter 
dated 20-8-1971 giving a list of witnesses to be examined 
on behalf of the employee (Ex. M-2). The third witness 
mentioned therein was one Sri R. Sankaralingam, working 
in the Kanir branch, Pursuant to Ex, M-2, the Enquiry 
Officer requested Sri R, Sankaralingam to attend the en- 
quiry (Vide Ex, M-3). Sri Sankaralingam attended the en- 
quiry and gave evidence on behalf of Sri S. Venugopal. It 
is not denied that Sri Sankaralingam came to Madras from 
Karur and deposed at the domestic enquiry on 20-10-1971 
and 21-10-1971, The claim now made by the union is that 
Sri Sankaralingam should be paid his travelling and daily 
allowances for the days he came to Madras to give evi- 
dence and to treat the period of his absence as duty. It 

is averred in the claim statement of the union that since it 
was the Enquiry Officer who called Sri Sankaralingam for 
examination at the enquiry, the bank should bear all the 
expenses incurred by him for appearing at the enquiry and 
should also terat his period of absence as on duty. It is 
stated that all the others who gave evidence were treated 
as on duty and that this amounts to discrimination and un- 
fair labour practice. It is further alleged that whenever 
any member of the staff of the bank was examined for an 

enquiry held by the bank he would bo paid halting allowance 

and travelling allowance apart from treating his period of 
absence as on duty, and that this has become a condition 
of service of an employee which cannot be altered without 
following the relevant provisions of the Industrial Dispute# 
Act. 

(3) The contention of the union that Sri Sankaralingam 
was called by the Enquiry Officer to give evidence and that 
he would not have attended the enquiry, but for such re- 
quest is not tenable. It is clear from Exs. M-2 and M-3 
that it was only because the delinquent employee wanted 
(o examine Sri Sankaralingam among others on his behalf 
that the Enquiry Officer asked him to appear for the en- 
quiry. In other words, he was examined at the instance 
of the union on behalf of the delinquent employee at the 
enquiry. The Enquiry Officer had merely forwarded the 
request of the union. In a domestic enquiry, it is for the 
charge-sheeted worker to take steps to produce witnesses 
before the Enquiry Officer. Here, the Enquiry Officer 
appears to have assisted the delinquent employee by writing 
a letter like Ex. M-3. It cannot therefore be said that Sri 
Sankaralingam came to give evidence at the domestic en- 
quiry as he was asked to do so by the bank. 

(4) It is further contended in the claim statement that 

out of the six witnesses examined at the enquiry, all except 
Sri R. Sankaralingam were treated as on duty and that 
therefore this is discrimination. But. the other witnesses 
were working in Madras, the place of enquiry and therefore 
they appear to have been granted permission. The pre- 
vailing practice in the bank appears to be that only witnesses 
who are examined for the bank at a domestic enquiry are 
entitled to duty leave and halting and travelling allowances. 
It is urged for the union that the denial of such allowances 
and leave to a witness examined for a delinquent employee 
at the domestic enquity amounts to discrimination and un- 
fair labour practice. This contention cannot be accepted, 
Tn paragraph 17-12 of the settlement dated 14-12-1966 
(Ex. M-6), which is still in force, the procedure for any 

disciplinary action against an employee has been laid down. 
Para, 17.12(d) which is relevant for our purpose is as 
follows:- — 

"If the representative defending the employee is an 
employee of the bank at an outstation branch 

within the same State, he shall be relieved on 
special leave (on full pay and allowances) to re- 

present the employee and be paid one return fare. 
The class of fare to which he will be entitled 
would be the same as while travelling on duty. In 
case of any adjournment ut the instance of the 
bank, he may be asked to resume duty and if so, 
will be paid fare for the consequential journey. 
He shall also be paid 50 per cent of the halting 
allowance for the period he stays at the place of 
the enquiry for defending the employee as also 

for the days of the journeys which are undertaken 
at the bank’s cost.” 



1062 


[Part II 


THE GAZETTE OF 1NDDIA : MARCH 


(5) The management had, therefore, agreed to grant 
travelling and halting allowance only to an employee re- 
presentative defending the employee and to treat his absence 
as on duty leave. This concession was not extended to the 
witnesses examined on behalf of the employee. The 
payment of duty and halting allowances to each and every 
witness examined for the employee would undoubtedly 
entail extra financial commitment to the bank and it would 
have been provided for if it was the intention of the manage- 
ment to make such payment to the employees’ witnesses. 
Under paragraph 19.6 of the settlement the union has agreed 
not to raise any further demand involving financial outlay 
affecting the bank. In view of the terms of settlement, the 
claim of the union for payment of travelling and halting 
allowances for Sri Sankaralingam cannot be allowed and 
no question of discrimination would arise. 

(6) It is however argued for the union that in as much 
as paragraph 17-12 of the settlement is silent about pay- 
ment of allowances to witnesses deposing for the employee, 
it cannot be said that such payment is precluded by the 
settlement. It is also urged that the jursdiction of an 
industrial tribunal for the adjudication of an industrial dis- 
pute is not invoked merely for the enforcement of contrac- 
tual rights and liabilities of the parties to the dispute and 
that the Tribunal’s function is to advance the cause of social 
justice, and that it can create new rights and obligations 
between the employer and the employee which it considers 
essential for keeping industrial peace and that therefore it 
can impose an obligation on the management to pay the 
allowances asked for, 

(7) I am unable to agreed with the contention that the 
settlement is not a bar to the claim of the union. At 
the time of the settlement, the parties must have been alive 
to the various aspects relating to the procedure and payment 
to be made to the parties in the domestic enquiry. If the 
parties wanted that the witnesses examined for the em- 
ployee should be given travelling allowances and his ab- 
sence from duty treated as duty leave it would have certainly 
been so provided in the settlement. Tt cannot be said that 
it had escaped the notice of the parties at the time of 
the settlement. Therefore, by providing for the payment 
of travelling and halting allowances only to those represent- 
ing the employee at the domestic enquiry, it should be 
taken that no other payment was agreed to be made by 
the employer to the employee for the purpose of the 
domestic enquiry. 

(8) It is no doubt true that it is open to an industrial 
tribunal to create now rights and obligations between the 
parties as in settling the disputes between the employer 
and the workmen, the function of the Tribunal is not con- 
fined to administration of justice in accordance with law. 
But this jurisdiction is not arbitrary. In Punjab National 
Bank, Ltd,, Vs. Industrial Tribunal, Delhi, and othcrsO). 
His Lordship Justice S. K. Das observed as follows at 
page 458:— 

“It may be conceded that the jurisdiction of an in- 
dustrial tribunal is not invoked for the enforcement 
of mere contractual rights and liabilities of the 
parties to the dispute referred to the tribunal for 
adjudication; its jurisdiction in the matter of ad- 

judication of an industrial dispute is wider and 
more flexible. All the same, it is not an arbitrary 
jurisdiction; it may be readily conceded that an 
employee is as much entitled to a fair deal as 

an employer and he must be protected from victi- 
mization and unfair labour practice, but “social 
justice” does not mean that reason and fairness 
must always yield to the convenience of a party — 
convenience of the employee at the cost of the 

employer as in this case — in an adjudication pro- 

ceeding. Such one-sided or partial view is really 
next-of-kln to caprice or humour ” 

Now, when it is the duty of the employee to take steps to 
produce his witnesses at the domestic enquiry it will be un- 
fair, to ask the management to meet the expenses of his 
witnesses as that will amount to asking the bank to finance 
the employee in the domestic enquiry. That will not be proper. 
In fact it will be casting an onerous financial obligation on 
the bank, which deals with its ciistomcr’s money. The 

“( 1 ) 1 9 5TT-L.L jT-Page - 4 55~ (S.C") “ “ 


3, 1973/PHALGUNA 12, 1894 


contention in the claim statement that the payment of such 
allowances to the members of the saff when figuring as 
witnesses in a domestic enquiry has become a condition of 
service has rightly not been pursued before me. There 
is also no material or basis for this contention. 

(9) In the result 1 hold that the demands of the workman 
are not justified and pass an award accordingly. 

Dated, this 1st day of February, 1973.

AWARD ON PRELIMINARY OBJECTION 


By Order No. L-3601 1 /4/71-P&D dated 14-9-1971 the 
central Government in the Ministry of Labour and Rchabilb 
ration (Department of Labour and Employment) in exercise of 
the powers conferred by clause (d) of sub-section (1) of 
Section 10 of the LD. Act, 1947 (14 of 1947) referred to 
this Tribunal for adjudication an industrial dispute existing 
between the employers in relation the Stevedores at Mormu- 
gao Harbour, Mormugao and their workmen in respect of the 
matters specified in the Schedule as mentioned below : — 

"SCHEDULE 

Whether the demand of the gangmen marked as ‘1/ in 
the records of the Mormugao Dock Labour Board 
for being placed in the scale of Rs, 135-4-163-5-178 
with effect from the 1st January, 1969 in the light 
of the recommendations of the Centra] Wage Board 
for Port and Dock Workers, is justified ? If not, 
to what relief if any, are the workmen entitled ?” 

2 .. Messrs. V. S. Dempo and Company and 11 Other Steve- 
dores thereinafter referred to as 'the Employers’) have filed 
common written statement at Ex. 4/E raising preliminary 
objections to the maintainability of the reference, 

3. According to them : 

(i) This reference is bad in law and not maintainable as 

this Tribunal’s jurisdiction to adjudicate on a dispute 
regarding wages of any dock workers under the 
Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, is impliedly barred 
by reason of the provisions of the Dock Workers 
(Regulation of Employment) Act, 1948 (hereinafter 
referred to as ‘the Act') and the Mormugao Dock 
Workers (Regulation of Employment) Scheme 1965 
(hereinafter referred to as the ‘the Scheme’) 

(ii) Under the Act the Mormugao Dock Labour Board 

(hereinafter referred to as k thc Board’) is the only 
competent authority to determine the wages and 
conditions of service ol Dock Workers. 

(iii) The Board is an autonomous body competent to deter- 

mine and prescribe the wages, allowances and other 
conditions of service of the dock workers. The 
registered employers do not have the freedom of 
prescribing the rates of wages and other terms and 
conditions of service. The power to determine wages 
and other service conditions is exclusively within the 
ambit of the powers of the Board. It is not open 
to this Tribunal nor has this Tribunal, any juris- 
diction to determine the wages and service condi- 
tions of any dock workers. 

(iv) Jurisdiction of the Tribunal to create a new contract 

between disputed parties can only be exercised if 
the employer is himself capable of contracting terms 
and conditions of employment. Tn the present case 
the employers have no jurisdiction nor power by 
reason of the said Act and Scheme to determine and 
fix wages and other service conditions, and there- 
fore, there can be no adjudication of the present 
dispute as the employers concerned are not compe- 
tent to create any contract. 

4. The Goa Dock Labour Union (hereinafter referred as 
‘the Union’) has filed written statement at Ex. 7/W regarding 
preliminary objection raised by the employers, 

5. According to the Union : 

(i) The reference is not bad In law. Tt is maintainable. 
This Tribunal has jurisdiction to adjudicate upon 
any dispute regarding wages of any dock worker 
under the Act. This jurisdiction is not impliedly 


MARCH 3, 1973/PHALGUNA 12, 1894 1063 


barred by reason of the provisions of the Act and 
the Scheme as alleged. 

(ii) The present dispute is a dispute between the Stevedores 

on one hand and their employees on the other. The 
Stevedores carry on the Stevedoring industry and 
therefore the dispute between the Stevedores and 
their workmen is an industrial dispute. When such 
dispute is referred to an Industrial Tribunal for 
adjudication, it has jurisdiction to consider and adju- 
dicate upon the demands made by the workmen 
against their employers. The Provisions of the Act 
and Scheme do not bar the authority of an Indus- 
trial Tribunal to adjudicate upon an industrial dispute 
between the Dock Workers and their employers. 

(iii) The Board constituted under the Scheme is not the 

employer of the Dock Workers. The said Board is 
a Statutory Board charged with the duty of adminis- 
tering the scheme, the object of which is to ensure 
the greater regularity of the employment for Dock 
Workers and to ensure that adequate number of 
Dockworkers arc available for efficient performance of 
the Dock work. Though the Board is competent to 
determine and prescribe the wages and allowances 
and other conditions of service of the Dock Workers, 
it has no powers and authority to adjudicate upon 
the dispute between Dock Workers and their emp- 
loyers relating to wages find allowances and other 
conditions of service. It has only the power to 
endeavour to settle disputes about which a request 
for adjudication has been made to the Central 
Government by the parties concerned and to report, 
to the Govt, the results of such endeavours. This 
clearly shows that there can be an adjudication of 
a dispute between the Dock Employers and their 
Dock Workers. The Board has no power to adjudi- 
cate upon them. The power of adjudication lies 
only with an Industrial Tribunal. 

(iv) The wages and allowances determined by the Board 
can be a cause of a dispute and either the Employers 
or the Dock Workers can raise an industrial dispute, 
with regard to the wages and allowances determined 
by the Board. The power and the authority of an 
Indstrial Tribunal to adjudicate upon an industrial 
dispute between the Dock workers and their emp- 
loyers is not taken away by the provisions of Sec- 
tion 8 of the Dock Workers (Regulation of Employ- 
ment) Act, 1946 Clauses 42 and 43 of the Scheme 
clearly show that the rales of wages and allowances 
etc. fixed by the Board form an implied condition 
of contract between the registered Dock Workers 
and the Registered Employers. An industrial adju- 
dication has the power to vary the said contract 
whether implied or express when an industrial dispute 
in respect of any term of the same is referred to 
It for adjudication. 

6. The Labour Officer for Mormugao Dock Labour Board, 
has filed written statement at Ex. 8/E. 

7. According to him : — 

(i) Mormugao Dock Labour Board is neither a necessary 

party nor a proper party to the proceedings. 

* 

(ii) The Board is a statutory body constituted by the 

Central Government in exercise of powers conferred 
on it under Section 5A of the Dock Workers (Regu- 
lation of Employment) Act, 1948 read with Rule 
3 to 6 of the Dock Workers (Regulation of Employ- 
ment) Rules 1962. 

(ill) The Board is charged with the responsibility of admi- 
nistering the provisions of statutory scheme known 
as Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of Employ- 
ment) Scheme 1965 framed by the Central Govern- 
ment in exercise of powers conferred on it under 
Sub-section (1) of Section 4 of the Act. The Board 
Is an Autonomous body competent to determine 
and prescribe the Wages, allowances and other ser- 
vice conditions of the Dock Workers under Clause 
8(f) of the Scheme. 

(lv> The Board is neither an Employer of the Workmen 
concerned in the reference nor carrying on any 
Industry so as to attract the provisions of Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947. 



1064 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3, 1973'/PHALGUNA 12, 1894 


[P ART II— 


(v) The Supreme Court In Visakhapatnam Dock Labour 
Board Vs. Stevedores Association, 1970-1, LU, P. 46, 
has clearly held that the Dock Labour Board is 
neither an Employer nor carrying on any Industry 
go as to attract the provisions of the Industrial Dis- 
putes Act, 1947. This Tribunal has no jurisdiction 
to pass any award saddling the Board with any 
liability of any kind. 

(vi) The scheme applied to two categories of workers for 

purposes of conferring certain benefits and status. 
They are Gang workers and Winchmen (as per Sche- 
dule 1 of the Scheme and clause 17(2) of the 
Scheme.) 

(vii) As there is no category of Gangleaders in the scheme, 

the demand of the workmen for increased wages is 
ill conceived and as such not maintainable. On 
account of this the reference is bad in law. 

(viii) Any award or order granting the increased wages to 
workmen in the reference would amount to creation 
of the separate category of Gangleaders and amend- 
ment to the Schedule 1 of the Scheme which is a 
power vested with the Central Government. This 
Tribunal therefore has no power to amend the Sche- 
dule, and as such has no jurisdiction to adjudicate 
this dispute. 

8. The workmen concerned in this reference who arc leaders 
of Gangs which work for Stevedores of Mormugao have filed 
common written statement at Ex. 1/W on 22-10-1971 on 
merit. It is only after their written statement, the Stevedores 
filed their written statement at Ex. 4/E raising preliminary 
objection regarding jurisdiction of this Tribunal.

(iii) As per order. 


“The dock workers employed at the Visakhapatnam port 
raised a dispute for payment of bourn for the yean 
1964-65, 1965-66 and 1966-67. Tho *ame wa* 
referred for adjudication. The basil of tho workers’ 
claim was that inasmuch as the work performed 
by them was as required under tho Dock Worker* 
(Regulation of Employment) Act, 1948, and the 
Visakhapatnam Dock Worker* (Regulation of Emp- 
loyment) Scheme which also govern* the other port*, 
such as Madras, Cochin, Bombay and Calcutta and 
that the bonus i* paid to the employees working in 
the respective ports under agreement* entered into 
between them and the respective stevedores, they should 
be also paid bonus in the same, way. The claim for 
payment of bonus was baaed on tnmover of work 
calculated in term* of tonnage. 

While resisting tho claim on merits, the ■tevedorea, Asto- 
ciation and the stevedore* who are registered emp- 
loyers and members of the said association contended 
that the dock workers are the workmen of dock 
labour board as all the ingredient* of master and 
servant existed between them, that the dock workers 
are not tho employee* of th stevedores, ad there- 
fore the dock workers could not claim bonus against 
the stevedores or their association. On the other 
hand the appellant board contended that it was a 
statutory body constituted under the Dock Workers 
(Regulation of Employment) Act 1948, and governed 
by the scheme frarped thereunder and its functions 
under the Act and" scheme could not be character- 
ized as “carrying’* on of an “industiw” so as to 
attract the provisions of the Industrial Disputes Act, 
1947, As the stevedores carry on the stevedoring 
industry, they alone arc liable to pay bonus to the 
dock workers. It was further contended that it was 
not liable to pay bonus and that It was not a 
necessary party to the proceeding* and that inasmuch 
as the stevedores* association by itself in an admini-i 
trative body constituted under a scheme framed 
under the Act, it had no further part to play. 

The industrial tribunal held that the demand for bonus 
by the workmen for the three years in question w r as 
justified and the same had to be paid by the appel- 
land-board on tonnage basis for the years in ques- 
tion. 

In th* justtmt app#al hy *p##itl l*av« it w** ##it#ad*d 

on behalf of th# appellant- h##r* that th# Trtbuial 
had acted illegally and without jOrisdicti## in making 



1065 


Sbc. 3(U)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3, 1973/PHALGCNA 12, 1894 

■JBIIIi— MM iimi .. i j L -j .ii. ■_ HI if! . . T" f M^. ~ J71T- 


the bo*rd liable for payment of bonu* when the 
claim of the workmen for such payment wai against 
stevedores' association and its members while the 

stevedores' association and the stevedores contended 
that the appellant- board along is liable as the work- 
ers' claim was against it and the direction in the 
award of the industrial tribunal making the appellant- 
board liable to pay bonus was correct. Alter con- 
sidering the pleadings of parties, held that the state- 
ments contained in them clearly showed that the 
claim for payment of bonus by the dock workers 
was essentially and in the main directed against the 
stevedores' association and its members, and that 

the large profits made by the appellant-board had no 
relevance at all. Having observed that the claim 
of workmen for bonus was against the stevedores 
and the basis for such claim would be the one 
adopted at the other ports where bonus is paid 
the workmen pursuant to agreements between the 

dock workers and the stevedores of respective ports, 
the industrial tribunal should not have held that 

the appellant-board was liable to pay bonus to the 
dock workers. 

It was further contended on behalf of the appellant-board 
that having due regard to the provisions of the Act 
and the scheme and the functions discharged by it, 
the tribunal should have held that there was no 
employer employee relationship between the appel- 
lant-board and the dock workers and as such it 
could not be made liable. On the other hand, it 
was contended on behalf of the stevedores' associa- 
tion and its member stevedores that there existed 
the relationship of employer-employee between the 
appellant-board and the dock workers, as recruitment 
and registration of the dock labour force, fixation of 
wages and dearness allowance, payment of work- 
men's compensation, taking of disciplinary action and 
prohibiting, the employment of workmen who are 
not registered with it arc all done by the appellant- 
board. 

While accepting the contentions of the appellant-board 
and rejecting the contentions of the stevedores and 
their association, the salient features of the Dock 
Worken (Regulation of Employment) act and the 
scheme framed thereunder vftre considered. The 
Dock Workers (Regulation of Employment) Act, 
1948, and the Visaknapatnam Dock Workers (Regu- 
lation of Employment) Scheme gave a true picture 
of the nature of the functions and the duties the 
appellant-board discharged in respect of the work 
carried on in the Foil. It was held that it is 
evident from that that the board is a statutory body 
charged with the duty of administering the scheme, 
the object of which is to ensure greater regularity of 
the employment for dock workers and to secure that 
adequate number of dock workers are available for 
efficient performance of dock work. The board 
is an autonomous body competent to determine and 
prescribe the wages, allowance and other conditions 
of service of the dock workers. The purport of the 
scheme is held to be that the entire body of workers 
should be under the contraland supervision of the 
appellant-board. The registered employers are allot- 
ted monthly workers by the administrative body 
and the administrative body supplies whenever neces- 
sary the labour force to the stevedores (registered 
employers) from the reserve pool. The workmen who 
are allotted to the registered employers are to do 
the work under the control and supervision of the 
registered employers and to act under their directions. 
The registered employers pay the wages due to the 
workers to the administrative body of the Dock 
Labour Board and the latter in turn as agent of the 
registered employers pay them over to the concern- 
ed workmen. The definition of the expressions 
"Employer" under Cl. 2(b) and "dock worker" under 
Cl. 2(c), and the definition of the terms "dock emp- 
loyer" and "monthly worker" in Cls. 3(g) and 3(k), 
respectively, and the obligation cost on the registered 
dock workers to carry out his duties when allotted to 
work under a registered employer (to carry out his 
dutie*) in accordance with the direction* of the 
tatar Mid the provisions contained in Cl. 37(5) of 
tfc* tahwaat refardin payment by the registered emp- 
loyer to tfee administrative body of the gross wages 


due to tha dock worker ami the implied condition 
of the contract between tie reglaterea dock worker* 
ajid the registered employer under Cl, 40 read along 
with other provision* of the scheme regarding the 
functions of the board lead to the conclusion that 
the appellant-board could not be considered to be the 
employer of the dock workmen and there ii 
no relationship of master and servant bet- 
ween the two, li waa further held that 
some of the circumstances such as recruitment and 
registration of the dock labour force, fixation of 
wages and dearness allowance, payment of work- 
men'* compensation, taking of disciplinary action 
and prohibition against employment of workmen 
who arc not registered did not establish the rela- 
tionship of employer-employee between the appel- 
lant board and the dock workers. 

Further applying the principles laid down in Gymkhana 
Club case (1967-11 LLJ 720) in order to sec whether 
the appellant Dock Labour Board could be an 
"industry" within the meaning of that term under 
S, 2(0 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, it was 
held that it could not be said that the appellant 
board functioning under the Act and the scheme 
carries on any industry so as to attract the provi- 
sions of the Industrial Disputes Act. It was, there- 
fore, held that the caim for bonus could be met 
only from the actual employer in respect of any 
industry. The appellant could neither be the em- 
ployer, nor could it be said that it carries on any 
industry, and therefore the tribunal was wrong in 
directing the appellant-board to pay bonus for the 
years in question. The award of the industrial 
tribunal was set aside and the matter was remanded 
back to the tribunal to consider the claim of the 
workmen against the stevedores' association and its 
members who arc all parties to the reference." 

15. There can be no doubt from the above mentioned 
Supreme Court ruling that the Board is an autonomous 
body competent to determine and prescribe the wages, allow- 
ance and other conditions of service of the Dock Workers, 
This ruling also lays down that the Board cannot be con- 
sidered as the Employer of the Dock Workers and there in 
no relationship of master and servant between the two. It 
also lays down that the Board’s function under the Act and 
the Scheme is not to carry on any industry so as to attract 
the provision of the I.D. Act, 1947. 

16. According to Shri Onkar&ppa; — 

(i) The Scheme applies to two categories for purposes 
of confering certain benefits and status. They are 
Gangworkers and Winchmen (as per Schedule I of 
the Scheme and Clause 17(2) of the Scheme.) 

(ii) As there is no category of Gangleaders in the 
Scheme, the demand of the workmen for increased 
wages is illconceived and as such not maintainable. 

(iii) Any award or order granting the increased wages 
to workmen in the reference would amount to 
creation of separate category of Gangleaders and 
amendment to the Schedule I of the Scheme, which 
is a power vested with the Central Government 
only. This Tribunal has no power to amend the 
Schedule and as such has no jurisdiction to adju- 
dicate this reference. 

17. Para. 3 of the Gangworkers (Gang Leadcrs)’s writ- 
ten statement in Ex, 1/W is as follows; — 

"3. By its Resolution No. 318 dated 25-4-1970, the 
Mormugao Dock Labour Board resolved that the

19. Learned Advocate Shri Rele for the Stevedores con- 
tends that this Tribunal has no power to determine the 
wages, allowance and conditions of service of Dock Workers 
because the power to determine wages and conditions of 
service of Dock Workers is exclusively within the ambit of 
the power of the Board under the provisions of the Dock 
Workers (Regulation of Employment) Act, 1948 and the 
Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of Employment) 
Scheme 1965, framed thereunder, and that on account of 
this, this tribunal has no jurisdiction to adjudicate Upon the 
dispute between the stevedores and the Dock Workers re- 
garding their wages etc. In support of this contention 
Shri Rele relies on clauses 8(f) and 42(a) of the Scheme 
and two rulings viz. i) Ruling of Patna High Court in the 
case between Sarat Chattarjee and Co. (Private) ltd. and 
others and the Central Govt. Industrial Tribunal, Dhanbad 
and others reported in 1963, I,LLJ, Page 76 and (2) Ruling 
of Supreme Court in the case between Vegoils Private Ltd. 
and the workmen reported in 1971, II,LLJ, page 567, rele- 
vant portion at page 579, para. 44. 

20. It is contended on behalf of the Union that the 
power and authority of an Industrial Tribunal to adjudicate 
upon an industrial dispute between the Dock Workers and 
their Employers is not taken away by the provisions of 
Section 8 of the Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of 
Employment) Scheme 1965, that clauses 42 and 43 of the 
Scheme clearly show that the rates of wages allowances etc. 
fixed by the Board form an implied condition of contract 
between the registered Dock Workers and the Registered 
Employers, and that an Industrial Tribunal has the power 
to vary the said contract whether implied or express when 
an industrial dispute in respect of any term of tne same is 
referred to it for adjudication. 

21. According to the Union: — 

(i) the Mormugao Dock Labour Board constituted under 
the Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of 
Employment) Scheme 1965 is not the employer of 
the Dock Workers. 

(ii) the said Labour Board is a statutory Board charged 
with the duty of administering the scheme, the 
object of which is to ensure the greater regularity 
of employment for Dock Workers and to ensure 
that adequate number of Dock Workers are avail- 
able for the efficient performance of the Dock 
Work. 


(iii) though the Board is competent to determine and 

prescribe the wages and allowances and other 

conditions of service of the Dock Workers, it has 
no power and authority to adjudicate upon the dis- 
pute between the Dock Workers and their 

Employers relating to wages and Allowances and 
other conditions of service. 

(iv) it has only the power to endeavour to settle dispute 
about which a request for adjudication has been 
made to the Central Government by the parties 
concerned and report to the Government the results 
of such endeavours, 

(v) this clearly shows that there can be an adjudication 
of a dispute between the Dock Employers and 
and their Dock Workers, that the Board has no 
power to adjudicate Upon them, that the power of 
adjudication lies only with an Industrial Tribunal 
to whom such a dispute is referred for adjudication 
by the Central Government and that the wages 
and allowance determined by the Board can be a 
cause of a dispute and, 

(vi) either the Employers or the Dock Workers can 
raise an industrial dispute with regard to the wages 
and allowances determined by the Board. 

22. Clause 8(f) of the Scheme lays down that the Board 
in meeting shall be responsible for dealing with all matters 
of policy and in particular may determine the wages, allow- 
ances and other conditions of service and refix the guaran- 
teed minimum wages In a month after annual review. 

23, It is clear from clause 8(f) that the Board may deter- 
mine the wages, allowances and other conditions of service 
of Dock Workers. This clause does not lay down that the 
Board shall determine the wages. 

24, Shri Rele contends that clause 8(f) has to be read 
nlongwith clause 42(a) of the Scheme and that on reading 
the clauses together it will be clear that the word ‘may’ in 
clause 8 will have to be construed as ‘shall’. 

25. Shri Sowani on tfio other hand contends that if two 
words appear in the same sentence or clause, the intention 
of the legislature is clear. According to him, inasmuch as 
two words ‘shall’ and ‘may’ appear in clause 8 of the Scheme, 
the word ‘may’ will have to be construed as permissive and 
not as ‘shall’ Lc. binding. If the legislature wanted that the 
Board shall determine the wages, it would have used the 
word ‘shall’ and not ‘may’. 

26. There is much force in what Shri Sowani says. As 
two words ‘shall’ and ‘may’ appear in the same clause 8 of 
the scheme, the intention of the legislature is that the Board 
‘may’ or 4 -niay not’ determine wages. It can be inferred 
from this that exclusive jurisdiction for determining wages 
etc. has not been given to the Board. In any case the 
power to adjudicate the dispute regarding wages etc. between 
the Stevedores and the Dock Workers cannot be said to have 
been taken away by this clause. 

27. Clause 8(o) of the Scheme lays down that the Board 
in particular may endeavour to settle disputes about which- a 
request for adjudication has been made to the Central Gov- 
ernment by the parties concerned and report to the 
Government the results of such endeavours. 

28. In the case reported in 1963, I, LI J, page 76, between 
Sarat Chatterjcc and Co. (Pvt.) Ltd. and others and the 
Central Government Industrial Tribunal, Dhabad and others 
before the Patna High court and relied upo by Shri Rele 
it is held as follows: — 

“Sub-clause (0) of Cl. 8 of the Scheme no doubt 
ostulates that in spite of the scheme there could 
e disputes for which a reauest for adjudication 
could be made to the Central Government by the 
parties concerned and the Government in its turn 
will have to refer the industrial dispute for adjudi- 
cation by an appropriate Tribunal. But this sub- 
clause would not cover the disputes which arc within 
the exclusive jurisdiction of the Board, under 
Cls. 8(f) and 41 of the Scheme." - (Calcutta 

Scheme).” - 



Sec. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3, 1973/PHALGIJNA 12, 1894 


29. Tn view of the above observations of the Patna High 
Court there can be no doubt that in spite of the Scheme 

Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of Employment) 
Scheme 1965 there could be industrial dispute for which a 
request for adjudication could be made to the Central 
Government by the parties concerned and the Government 
in its turn will have to refer the industrial dispute for adjudi- 
cation by an appropriate Tribunal. 

30. In the present case I have already observed that clause 
8(f) does not give exclusive jurisdiction to the Board for 
determining the wages etc. What clause 8(f) shows is that 
the Board in particular may determine the wages etc. As 
the expression used in the clause is ‘may’ I am of the view 
that the jurisdiction of the Tribunal to adjudicate upon the 
industrial dispute regarding wages etc. of the Dock Workers 
is not taken away by this clause. 

31. In the Patna High Court ruling reported in 1963, 
I,LLJ, Page 76 it is observed that the workers in the Reserve 
Pool arc considered the employees of the Board but in view 
of the Supreme Court ruling referred to in para. 14 above, 
the Board is not the employer of the dock workers and 
it is not carrying on any industry. 

32. Shri Rele relies on para. 44 at page 579 of the Supreme 
Court ruling reported in 1971, II.LLJ, page 567. That 
paragraph is as follows:— 

“44. The legality of the direction given by the Indus- 
trial Tribunal abolishing contract labour in respect 
of loading and unloadig from May 1, 1971 can 
also be considered from another point of view. The 
Central Act, as mentioned earlier had come into 
force on February 10, 1971. Under S. 10 of the 
said Act the jurisdiction to decide matters connected 
with prohibition of contract labour is now vested in 
the appropriate Government. Therefore, with effect- 
from February 10, 1971, it iB only the appropriate 
Government that can prohibit contract labour by 
following the procedure and in accordance with 
the provisions of the Central Act. The Industrial 
Tribunal, in the circumstances will have no juris- 
diction, though its award is dated November 20, 
1970, to give a direction in that respect which 
’ becomes eforceablc after the date of the coming 
into force of the Central Act. Tn any event, such 
a direction contained in the award cannot bo en- 
dowed from a date when abolition of contract 
labour can only be done by the appropriate Gov- 
ernment in accordance with the provisions of the 
central Act. In this view also it must be held that 
the direction of the Industrial Tribunal abolishing 
contract labour with effect from May 1, 1971, re- 
garding loading and unloading cannot be sus- 
tained. 

33. It appears from the facts of the above ruling that 
jurisdiction to decide matters connected with prohibition of 
contract labour was vested with the Appropriate Government 
under section 10 of the Central Act. There is however no 
such provision under which the power to adjudicate the 
dispute regarding wages etc. between the Stevedores and 
the Dock Workers has been vested with the Board, Clause 
8(f) Of the Mormugao Dock Workers (Regulation of 
Employment) Scheme is only a permissive and not manda- 
tory. I am therefore of the view that the ruling in respect 
of abolition of Contract Labour relied upon by Shri Rele 
does not apply to the facts of the present case. 

34. Shri Rele contends that the jurisdiction of an Indus- 
trial Tribunal to create a new contract between disputed 
parties can only be exercised if the employer is himself 
capable of contracting terms and conditions of employment, 
that in' the present case the employers (Stevedores) have no 
jurisdiction nor pow r er by reason of the Act and the Scheme 
to- determine and fix wages and other service conditions of 
the Dock Workers and that on account of this, this Tribunal 
has no- jurisdiction to adjudicate upon the present dispute. 

-35: Clauses 42 and 43 of the Scheme show that wages 
etc. fixed by the Board* form an implied condition of the 
contract between a registered dock worker and registered 
employer. An Industrial Tribunal has power to vary the 
said contract between the stevedores and Dock Workers 


whether implied or express when an industrial dispute in 
respect of any terms of it is referred for adjudication. Hence 
the contention raised by Shri Rele fails. 

36. Registered Dock Workers are supplied to the Steve- 

dores by the Board. It is clearly observed by the Lordships 
of Supreme Court in para 24 on page 55 in the case bet- 
ween Visakhapatnam Dock Labour Board Vs. Stevedores' 
Association reported in 1970, I,LLJ, as follows: 

“24. All these circumstances in our opinion, prtrna facie 
establish that the Board cannot be considered to 
be the employer of the Dock Labour workmen. In 
fact the various provisions referred to in the 
Scheme, clearly show that the registered employer 
to whom the labour force is allotted by the Board 
is the employer whose work of loading or unload- 
ing of ships is done by the dock workers allotted 
to them." 

37. In view of the above observations of the Supreme 
Court it is clear that the Registered Dock Workers are the 
employees of the Stevedores to whom they are allotted for 
work. Hence the dispute between the Dock Workers and 
the Stevedores who are carrying on business which is an 
industry, will be an industrial dispute within the provisions 
of the I.D. Act, 1947. Hence the dispute k between the Steve- 
dores and the Dock Workers can be referred to an Industrial 
Tribunal by the Government under the provisions of the 
l.D. Act, 1947. 

38. In short, it will be clear from the above discussions 
that this Tribunal has jurisdiction to adjudicate the dispute 
between the Stevedores and the Gang Leaders marked as 
‘L’ and that this reference is not bad in law. I therefore 
pass the following order. 

ORDER 

(i) It is hereby declared that this Tribunal has jurisdic- 
tion to adjudicate the present dispute between the 
Stevedores and Dock Workers in question, and 
that the present reference is not bad in law. 

(ii) Reference is fixed for taking further steps. 

, (iii) Award on preliminary objection is made acc6rd- 
ingly. 

N. K. VANI, Presiding Officer.

2. After the receipt of the reference by this Tribunal notices were issued to the parties to file their written statement, 

tn pursuance of this notice the employers In relation to the management of Messrs. Timblo Private Limited, Margao, (hereinafter 
referred to as ‘the employers 1 ) filed written statement Ex. t/B raising various contentions. 

3. The General Secretary of the Goa Mining Labour Welfare Union representing the workmen with the employers in their mines 
has filed written statement at Ex. 2/W raising various contentions, 

4. The employers have filed rejoinder in reply to the statement of claim filed by the Goa Mining Labour Welfare Union at 
Ex. 3/E. 


5. In the present case the employers held ' cx-parte ' en- 
quiries against all the employees mentioned above. Each 
employee was charged for going on illegal strike and un- 
authorised absence. These charges were held proved against 
each employee and each employee was dismissed by the 
authority concerned. 

6. The employers have produced all the enquiry papers 
in this case. 

7. The Union examined 12 workmen involved in this case 
and produced some documents. 

8. The employers produced number of documents in the 
cross-examination of each workers. 

9. On 5-14973, Shri George Vaz, General Secretary, Goa 
Mining Labour Welfare Union, Assonora, Bardes, Goa has 
given application at Ex. 114/W in this proceedings. Tt is as 

follows; — 

“May it please the Honourable Tribunal, 

The present dispute between the above named parties 
has been referred to this Honourable Tribunal by 
the Government of India, Ministry of Labour, 
Employment and Rehabilitation (Department of 
Labour and Employment) by its order No, 
24/69/69-LRIV dated 5-14970 for adjudication. 

The subject matter of the dispute arose as the first party 
Employer illegally and unjustly terminated the ser- 
vices of all their workmen during the period of 

52 G of T/72 — 16 


strike which had started on the demand for payment 
of Wage Board differences some time on the 2lst 
January, 1969, Subsequently the Government of 
India referred this dispute to adjudication and pro- 
hibited the strike of workmen. And although the 
workmen had withdrawn the strike they were not 
allowed to resume duty. 


me union had demanded reinstatement and filed a 
complaint before the Asstt. Labour Commissioner 
(C), Vasco-dfl;Gama who after satisfying himself 
had sent a failure of conciliation report on the 
basis of which the Government had made a refer- 
ence for adjudication of the dispute before thii 
Honourable Tribunal. 


It is however found that a formal dispute was not 
raised before the Employer Messrs. Timblo 
Pvt. Ltd., Margao on the subject matter of the pre- 
sent reference. In view of this the Union does 
not wish to prosecute the present reference without 
prejudice to its right to raise a fresh industrial dis- 
pute on the same cause of action and take such 
other legal steps that may be found necessary for 
a proper adjudication of the dispute which is pend- 
ing. 

It is prayed that the Honourable Tribunal may pass 
orders accordingly. 


Dated the 5th day of January, 1973;* 


10, On the receipt of this application 
to the employers to give their say. 


notice was issue* 



1070 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3, 1973/PHALGUNA 12, 1894 [Part Ti- 


ll, On 25-1-1973 Shri S.V. Cuncoliencer, through Shri 
G.M. Kothari, advocate for the employers, filed reply on 
behalf of the employers at Ex, 115/E, It is as follows: — 

“May it please the Hon’bJe Tribunal: 

The employer* beg to submit their reply to Union's 
application of 5th January, 1973 as follows: — 

1. As the Goa Mining Labour Welfare Union does not 

want to prosecute the present reference, it is pray- 
ed that the same may be dismissed and an award 
may be given rejecting the pray«r referred to in 
the Schedule of the Order of Reference. 

2. In view of the Union’s prayer, it is not necessary to 

deal with the other averments in the application and 
the employers reserve their right to make submis- 
sions as and when the same may be necessary and 
required. Without prejudice to it, the employers 
submit as follows: 

3. That the Union’s statement that they do not wish to 

prosecute the reference without prejudice to their 
right etc. is irrelevant and no such observations can 
be made or prayer accepted as on their own state- 
ments in the application, they admit that there was 
no demand made, there was no industrial dispute 
raised. The Hon'ble Tribunal will not therefore 
make any such observations or accept any such 
prayer except dismissal of reference at the prayer 
of Union for its non-prosecution, 

4. The employers do not admit the facts mentioned in 

para 1 to 2 of the application and deny that there 
was any illegal or unjust termination as alleged or 
otherwise or that there was any demand made by 
the workmen or any industrial dispute as referred 
had arisen or any of the actions of the conciliation 
officers or conciliation proceedings made by the 
Government was justified proper or legal. It is also 
denied that any demand for reinstatement was made 
as submitted or otherwise. AH such complaints or 
proceedings were without law. 

3. It is submitted that the move is designed to subvert 
the normal course of justice,” 

12. -In view of the application Ex. 114/W given by the 
Union referred to above, I have not referred to the conten- 
tions raised by both the parties while referring to their 
statements. 1 am also not mentioning the exhibits and de- 
tails of the documents and the names of the employees exa- 
mined by the Union and various documents produced by the 
Union and the employers. 

13. The General Secretary of the Goa Mining Labour 
We/fare Union has mentioned in his application Ex, 114/W 
that a formal dispute was not raised with the employers 
Messrs. Tim b to Pvt Ltd., Margao on the subject matter of 
the present reference. In view of this the following issues 
arise for consideration. 

(i) Whether this Tribunal has jurisdiction Lo entertain 
this reference? 

(ii) What order? 

14. My findings arc as follows: — 

(i) No 

(ii) As per order, 

REASONS 

Point Nos* 1 and ii 

15. The learned Advocate Shri Kothari for the employ- 
ers contends that this reference should be dismissed for 
want of prosecution without making any observations regard- 
ing the jurisdiction of the Court in respect of the tenability 
of the reference. This contention cannot be accepted. 

16. As the Union has brought to nhe notice of this Tri- 
bunal by application Ex. 114/W that a formal dispute was 
not raised with the employers Messrs. Timblo Pvt. Ltd. 
on the subject matter of the present reference, the questiou of 


jurisdiction arises in this case. As this question goes to the 
root of the case, this Tribunal has to decide the question of 
jurisdiction, 

17. The Union's prayer in its application Ex, 114/W to 
the effect that it does not want to prosecute the present refe- 
rence without prejudice to its right to raise a fresh industrial 
dispute on the same cause of action and take such other 
legal steps for a proper adjudication of the dispute which 
is pending because a formal dispute was not raised with the 
employers Messrs. Timblo Private Ltd. on the subject matter 
of the present reference means that the union wants to keep 
its right to raise a fresh industrial dispute with the employ- 
ers Messrs. Tibmblo Pvt. Ltd. on the same cause of action 
to be kept open while not proceeding with the present re- 
ference. In view of this Shri Kotharfs contention that this 
reference should be dismissed for want of prosecution 
without making any observations regarding the jurisdiction 
of the Court in respect of the tenability of the reference 
cannot be accepted. 

18. Shri Vaz for the Union relying on the Supreme Court 
ruling reported in 1968, I, LLJ, page 834 contends that this 
Tribunal has no jurisdiction to entertain this reference and 
that it may bo ordered accordingly. There is much force in 
this contention. 

19. On the admission of the Union given in its applica- 
tion Ex. 114/W as mentioned above, it, is crystal clear that 
the Union had not raised an industrial dispute with the em- 
ployers Messrs, Timblo Pvt. Ltd. in respect of the subject 
matter of the present reference before going to the Assistant 
Labour Commissioner (C) Vasco-da-Gama and before the 
Government referred this dispute to this Tribunal for adjudi- 
cation. As the dispute regarding reinstatement challenging 
the dismissals was not raised with the employers it cannot 
become an industrial dispute. 

20. The present reference has been made to this Tribunal 
by the Government under Section 10(1) (d) of the l.D. Act, 
1947. Section 10(1) (d ) of the l.D. Act, 1947 is as 
follows;—! 

“Where the appropriate Government is of the opinion 
that any industrial dispute exists or is apprehended 
it may at tiny time, by order in writing refer the 
dispute or any mailer appearing to be connected 
with, or relevant to the dispute, whether it relates 
lo anv matter specified in the Second Schedule or 
the Third Schedule to a Tribunal for adjudication.” 

2L As per Section 10(1) (d) of the l.D. Act, 1947, the 
appropriate Government can refer the dispute to this Tribunal 
if it is of the opinion that an industrial dispute exists. In 
Ihe present case, as no dispute regarding reinstatement 
challenging the dismissals was raised with the employers in 
ihe beginning, it is not an industrial dispute. As it is not 
an industrial dispute no references can be made and this Tri- 
bunal has no jurisdiction to enter Lain the same. 

22. In the case between Sindhu Resettlement Corporation 
Ltd. and Industrial Tribunal, Gujarat and other, reported in 
1968, T, LET, Page 834, their lordships of the Supreme Court 
have held as follows:— 

“On the facts of this case it is clear that the reference 
made by the Government was not competent. A 
mere demand to a Government without u dispute 
being raised by the workmen with their employer 
cannot become an industrial dispute ...” 

In view of this Supreme Court ruling referred to 
above, it is clear that the present reference is incompetent 
1 1 s it was not raised with the employers. 

23. When the Tribunal has no jurisdiction to entertain the 
reference, the Tribunal cannot decide other contentions 
raised by the parties. It cannot also make any observations 
regarding various other pleas and conientions raised in their 
written statements. It cannot give any direction lo either of 
the parties, when it has prima facie no jurisdiction to consi- 
der the matter. 

24. Ab this Tribunal has no jurisdiction, this /reference 
fails. I therefore pass the following order. 



Snc. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3, 1973/PHALGUNA 12, 1894 1071 


ORDER 

(i) Jt is hereby declared that this Tribunal has no juris- 
diction to entertain this reference. 

(ii) Reference is dismissed 

(iii) Award is made accordingly. 

(iv) No order as to costs. 

N. K. VANT, Presiding Officer.

New Delhi, the 22nd February, 1973 

S.O. 677. — WHEREAS the departmental! y run Govern- 
ment of India Presses under the Control of Ministry of 
Works and Housing (Chief Controller of Printing and 
Stationery, New Delhi), specified in Schedule I annexed 
(hereinafter refrred to as the said establishment have ap- 
plied for exemption under clause (b) of sub-section (1) of 
section 17 of the Employees’ Provident Funds and Family 
Pension Fund Act, 1952 (19 of 1952) (hereinafter referred 
to as the said Act); 

AND WHEREAS the employees of the said presses are 
in enjoyment of benefit in the nature of provident funds, 
pension or gratuity and the Central Government is of opi- 
nion that such benefits are on the whole not less favourable 
to such employees than the benefits provided under Em- 
ployees’ Provident Fund Scheme, 1952 (hereinafter referred 
to as the said Scheme) in relation to employees in any other 
establishment of a similar character; 

NOW, THEREFORE, in exercise of the powers confeired 
by clause (a) of sub-section (1) of section 17 of the said 
Act, and subject to the conditions specified in the Schedule II 
annexed hereto, the Central Government hereby exempts the 
said presses from the operation of all the provisions of the 
said Scheme and in pursuance of sub-section (3) of the said 
section 17, the Central Government hereby directs that the 
employer in relation to the said presses shall pay within 
fifteen days of the close of the month to the Employees 
Provident Fund, Inspection charges at the rate of 0,09 per 
cent (zero point zero nine per cent) of the pay (basic wages, 
dearness allowance, retaining allowance, if any, and cash 
value of food concession admissible thereon) for the time 
being payable to the employees of the said presses who 
would have become members under the said Scheme but 
for this exemption. 

SCHEDULE I 

1. Government of India Text Book Press, Chandigarh. 

2. Government of India Text Book Press, Bhubaneshwar. 



SEC.3(ii)] 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 3,1973/PHALGUNA 12,1894 107 


SCHEDULE II 

1. The employer shall submit such returns to the Re- 

gional Provident Fund Commissioner as the Cen- 
tral Government may, from time to time, pres- 
cri.be. 

2. The employer shall furnish to each employee an 

annual Statement of account or Pass Book. 

3. All expenses involved in the administration of the 

fund including the maintenance of accounts, sub- 
mission of accounts and returns, transfer of accu^ 
mutations, payment of inspection charges etc,, 
shall be borne by the employer. 

4. Where an employee who is already member of the 

Employees’ Provident Fund (Statutory Fund) or 
the Provident Fund of another exempted estab- 
lishment, is employed in his establish- 
ment, the employer shall immediately enroll him 
as a member of the fund of the establishment, and 
accept the past accumulations in respect of such em- 
ployee and credit to his account. 

5. The employer shall enhance the rate of provident 

fund contribution appropriately if the rate of pro- 
vident fund contributions for the class of cstab- 
ments in which his establishment falls is enhanced 
under the Employees' Provident Funds and Family 
Pension Fund Act, 1952 so that the benefits under 
the provident fund scheme of the establishment 
shall not become less favourable than the benefit 
provided under the Employees’ Provident Fund3 
and Family Pension Fund Act, 1952. 

6. Notwithstanding anything contained in the provident 

fund rules of the establishment if the amount pay 
able to any member, upon his ceasing to be an 
employee of the establishment or transferable on 
his transfer to any other establishment, by way of 
employer’s contributions plus interest thereon taken 
together with the amount, if any, payable under 
the Gratuity /Pension rules, be less than the 
amount that would be payable as employer’s and 
employees’ contribution together with interest 
thereon, if he were a member of the Provident 
Fund under the Employees’ Provident Fund 
Scheme, 1952, the employer shall pay the difference 
to the member as Compensation or Special Con- 
tribution. 

7. Where employees are governed by the provisions of 

General Provident Fund Rules, 1960 or Contri- 
butory Provident Fund Rules, 1962 (India), no 
amendment of the rules of the Provident Fund 
which will result in reduction of existing benefits 
cither directly or indirectly of such employees shall 
be made without consultation with the Department 
of Labour and Employment, Government of India. 

8. No amendment of the rules of the provident fund 

shall be made without the previous approval of 
the Regional Provident Fund Commissioner, Where 


any amendment is likely to affect adversely the in- 
terests of the employees, the Regional Provident 
Fund Commissioner, shall, before giving his ap- 
proval, give a reasonable opportunity to the em- 
ployees to explain their point of view.

S.O. 679. — In exercise of the powers conferred by sub- 
section (1) of section 83 of the Mines Act, 1952 (35 of 1952), 
the Central Government hereby exempts the persons em- 
ployed by the Oil and Natural Gas Commission on explora- 
tion drilling and production operations at Off-Shore Oil 
Exploration at Bombay High Structure from the operation 
of the provisions of Chapter VI of the said Act except 
section 36 and sections 40 to 48 (both inclusive) thereof, 
subject to the conditions, that the said persons, 

(i) shall not be employed for more than twelve hours 

on any day, 

(ii) shall not be employed for more than two weeks at 

a stretch, 

(iii) shall, after they have worked for two weeks at a 

stretch be granted rest for a period of not less 
than one week.
